src/2025/11/25/you-cant-tell.md: Simulation series showing that queue-length and time plots alone cannot reveal which parameter changed, whether cutting testers from three to two or making testing time depend on development time; dashboards need closer data.
src/2025/11/26/malice-and-randomness.md: Argues a simple view of a complex system can be misread; apparent backlog spikes were just random variation. The statistical analogue of Hanlon's Razor says do not attribute to action what random variation explains; use Shewhart charts.
src/2025/11/27/not-created-equal.md: Modifies the simulation to use priority stores, showing low-priority tasks pile up while testers keep up; draws a parallel to Plotly backlog, concluding ruthless labeling is the only workable response.
src/2025/11/28/what-changed.md: A new CEO examines bug-fix times and finds long-running bugs rising and completions falling, with no turnover or rewrite; poses the what-changed puzzle and promises an answer tomorrow.
src/2025/11/29/what-changed-revisited.md: Reveals a rework loop in the simulation, where testers send tasks back at high priority, explains the rising times and falling completions, but data alone cannot say why; explanation still requires investigation.
src/2025/11/30/observability.md: Refactors the simulation toward realistic data collection using a Log process and WorkLog context manager; notes roughly half the code collects data and the testing queue's rise and fall remains unexplained.

src/2025/12/03/you-have-to-cancel.md: Documents a three-hour SimPy debugging session showing that with AnyOf every unselected request must be canceled, or the developer stops receiving work; plans documentation pull requests.

src/2026/04/18/changing-minds.md: Argues beliefs are signals of group identity, so evidence fails through the backfire effect and motivated reasoning; meaningful regulation requires trusted messengers and reframing rather than more data.
src/2026/04/19/asimpy-improvements.md: Lists four micro-optimizations, dropping functools.partial, inlining the waiter, moving the exception check, and a lazy waiter slot, yielding two to twenty percent wall-clock gains at the cost of readability.
src/2026/04/19/big-tech-cocaine-cartels.md: Uses Narconomics to argue big tech and cartels solve the same business problems of brand, labor, and vertical integration by different means, and that regulation is society's immune reaction.
src/2026/04/20/big-tech-fraud.md: Applies Lying for Money to technology, showing Ponzi, long-firm, control, and accounting fraud patterns recurring in Silicon Valley business plans and incentives.
src/2026/04/21/big-tech-is-soviet.md: Argues standardization serves the center over the periphery, and that Amazon and Walmart run internal command economies, so the Soviet collapse reflected missing computation, not proof that markets always win.
src/2026/04/22/big-tech-as-church.md: Compares platforms to the medieval Church: the app-store cut is a tithe, deplatforming is excommunication, account suspensions are interdicts, and moderation claims mirror ecclesiastical jurisdiction.
src/2026/04/23/big-tech-as-payola.md: Links supermarket slotting fees and radio payola to platform self-preferencing, arguing disclosure labels are inadequate and that structural separation is what would actually work.
src/2026/04/24/big-tech-as-stasi.md: Argues privacy is historically contingent rather than natural, that platform surveillance rivals the Stasi but rests on unrefusable consent, and that governments piggyback on corporate data collection.
src/2026/04/25/big-tech-as-beauty.md: Argues platforms manufacture status insecurity and sell temporary relief like the beauty industry, profiting by making people feel perpetually not-enough, with the harshest burden falling on women.
src/2026/04/25/big-tech-as-fast-food.md: Compares platforms to McDonald's franchises, which own the real estate and take royalties on gross sales while participants bear the risk, with switching costs trapping them in place.
src/2026/04/25/cognitive-pollution-revisited.md: Argues industrial harm is often diffuse and cumulative rather than traceable, so firms externalize it, and that like tobacco, tech manufactures doubt; liability should follow a pollution model.
src/2026/04/25/software-taboos.md: Argues software engineering literature excludes workers' rights and ownership just as medicine once excluded error; taboos mark group boundaries and serve the profession's most powerful members.
src/2026/04/25/why-discrimination-persists.md: Argues Becker's prediction that competition eliminates discrimination is falsified; implicit bias, audit studies, and benefits to favored groups explain persistence, as in caste and apartheid.
src/2026/04/26/big-tech-enclosure-movement.md: Argues platforms converted the open web commons into walled gardens, repeating the enclosure movement's privatization of common land and the colonial dispossession of customary tenure.
src/2026/04/27/big-tech-as-sharecropping.md: Argues platform lock-in mirrors sharecropping and peonage, with debt-structured terms trapping participants, and that gig work echoes the putting-out system, the app acting as supervisory infrastructure.
src/2026/04/27/big-tech-as-standard-oil.md: Argues venture-funded platform competition is a tontine, and that Standard Oil, zaibatsu, and Bell show monopolies reconsolidate after breakup while Chicago-school antitrust enabled today's dominance.
src/2026/04/28/big-tech-as-penny-press.md: Traces ad-funded media to the penny press, whose sensationalism was rational business, and argues algorithmic engagement optimization now amplifies outrage because it holds attention.
src/2026/04/28/differential-legalization.md: Argues what gets legalized tracks race and commerce rather than harm, comparing alcohol, cannabis, Portugal's decriminalization, and the opioid versus crack responses.
src/2026/04/28/geography-of-industrial-harm.md: Argues industry relocates pollution to places with weak regulation; Bhopal, waste exports, and e-waste reveal the tech industry's hidden geography behind its placeless self-presentation.
src/2026/04/29/big-tech-department-store.md: Argues Amazon's private-label self-preferencing, like Sears before it, is structurally the same conflict as securities front-running, yet platform markets escape those financial rules.
src/2026/04/29/guns-in-america.md: Traces how a novel individual-rights reading of the Second Amendment and the radicalized post-1977 NRA, plus racialized enforcement, sustain American gun-policy stasis while other countries acted quickly.
src/2026/04/29/myth-of-meritocracy.md: Argues meritocracy began as satire, elite selection runs on class legibility rather than merit, audit studies reveal bias, and believing in merit widens pay gaps and blames the poor.
src/2026/04/29/pornography-debate.md: Surveys the pornography regulation cycle, feminist and anti-censorship positions, ambiguous harm research, and warns that age verification would cut off isolated queer youth.
src/2026/05/01/big-tech-as-danwei.md: Argues platform dependency echoes the Pullman company town and China's danwei, where infrastructure control makes exit nearly impossible and super-apps aim to become the work unit.
src/2026/05/01/labor-and-passion.md: Traces labor organizing across Britain, Germany, Australia, and South Africa, and argues the passion principle makes tech workers accept worse terms by concealing that they are workers.
src/2026/05/01/short-history-of-fads.md: Surveys bubbles from tulips to AI, arguing they grow from real phenomena plus greater-fool and narrative dynamics, so the question is who captures value and who absorbs the cost.
src/2026/05/02/evolution-of-morality.md: Argues LGBTQ+ rights and assisted dying show moral consensus shifts through changed facts, visibility, organized pressure, and legal strategy, offering a model for AI and privacy norms.
src/2026/05/02/intellectual-property.md: Argues intellectual property is an invented legal instrument expanded by industry lobbying, that software patents and pharmaceutical TRIPS serve incumbents, and that open source only partially resists.
src/2026/05/03/other-kinds-of-firms.md: Corporate forms encode who controls, benefits, and bears risk; surveys sole proprietorships, partnerships, cooperatives like Mondragón, German codetermination, Japanese keiretsu, Islamic waqf, and Indian HUF, arguing the dominant Delaware dual-class model serves founders and venture capital, not workers.
src/2026/05/04/free-speech-and-hate-speech.md: Compares US and European free speech traditions via Charlie Hebdo, blood libel, Rwanda radio, and Nazi propaganda; hate speech laws both protect minorities and get weaponized against them, and platform moderation adds unaccountable private governance.
src/2026/05/05/harmful-things.md: Facebook Papers show harm arose from ordinary processes, not villains; explains Milgram's agentic state, Bandura's moral disengagement, and Vaughan's normalization of deviance, arguing tech's ethics pledges leave incentive structures unchanged.
src/2026/05/05/moral-panic.md: Traces the video game panic from Columbine through comic books and rock music; research shows weak effects, cross-national evidence contradicts it, and regulation followed a recurring alarm-attribution-restriction script now replaying for AI.
src/2026/05/05/philanthrocapitalism.md: Gates, Buffett, and Carnegie show private giving shaping public policy without democratic legitimacy; tax-deductible foundations convert wealth into donor-controlled endowments, from the Flexner Report to K-12 reform and WHO influence.
src/2026/05/06/algorithmic-justice.md: COMPAS, facial recognition, welfare systems, Aadhaar, and SyRI show algorithms entrenching historical bias; Chouldechova proves fairness metrics conflict, and proprietary code defeats defendants' right to confront evidence.
src/2026/05/06/public-subsidy-private-profit.md: The iPhone's core technologies and drug science were publicly funded, then profits privatized; Mazzucato's critique contrasted with European revenue-sharing and China's state-directed model.
src/2026/05/06/tax-avoidance.md: Apple's Double Irish and Dutch Sandwich showed legal profit-shifting; OECD BEPS and the 15% minimum tax are weak, and Zucman estimates 40% of multinational profits shifted to havens.
src/2026/05/07/eight-hours-and-after.md: The eight-hour day was won by leverage, not persuasion: Melbourne stonemasons, Haymarket, and post-WWI fear of revolution; the Triangle fire plus organized capacity produced reform, a lesson for tech workers.
src/2026/05/08/lessons-from-ozone.md: Industry denied CFC science until the ozone hole; the Montreal Protocol worked via binding schedules, trade sanctions, and a transfer fund, but HFC substitutes traded ozone for climate damage.
src/2026/05/08/taming-tobacco.md: Tobacco manufactured doubt for forty years; the Master Settlement Agreement, Australian plain packaging, and ISDS abuse show a combination of litigation, regulation, and pressure eventually imposing costs.
src/2026/05/08/unsafe-at-any-algorithm.md: Nader, seatbelts, thalidomide, and Kelsey show industries' deny-attack-market playbook; regulation, not consumer choice, produced safety, an old argument tech now repeats against AI and privacy rules.
src/2026/05/09/big-tech-as-wrestling.md: Apple and Google's rivalry is kayfabe: scripted competition concealing the $20 billion search deal; regulators are referees whose fines are rounding errors, so hearings deliver spectacle, not structural change.
src/2026/05/09/cleaning-up-the-rivers.md: London's Great Smog, the Thames, the Cuyahoga, the Rhine, and Minamata show pollution ended only when law made it costly, not through conscience; markets don't price externalities without compulsion.
src/2026/05/09/cooperatives.md: Mondragón, Raiffeisen banks, Red Vienna housing, Amul, and Desjardins prove worker and consumer ownership scales; the claim that cooperatives can't work persists because those who benefit from shareholder firms write the business press.
src/2026/05/09/land-reform.md: US occupation redistributed Japanese, Korean, and Taiwanese land to counter communism, while Kerala did it democratically; unlike Stalin and Mao's collectivization, these created owner-farmers and enabled industrialization.
src/2026/05/10/big-tech-as-mlm.md: MLM recruiting mathematics means most participants subsidize a few; Uber, Amazon Marketplace, and social media replicate this, extracting value from participants who are product, salesperson, and customer at once.
src/2026/05/10/big-tech-scientology.md: Platforms' behavioral data collection resembles Scientology's auditing and preclear folders; both use Fair Game-style campaigns against critics and engineer progressive lock-in that makes leaving costly.
src/2026/05/10/big-tech-yakuza.md: Like the yakuza, platforms provide quasi-governmental services and extract rents through territorial control; Japan's anti-organized-crime laws show sustained political pressure can make such power a vulnerability.
src/2026/05/11/a-note-on-llms.md: Author explains he uses Claude to check redundancy and as a search engine; the post series grew from a 2017 idea to teach politics and economics to programmers, written while unemployed.
src/2026/05/11/ransom-as-a-business-model.md: Professional kidnapping and ransomware both became fee-for-service markets with insurers, negotiators, and reputational constraints; governments officially discourage paying while privately assisting.
src/2026/05/12/behavioral-economics.md: Kahneman and Tversky's framing, anchoring, availability, loss aversion, and hyperbolic discounting contradict the rational-agent model; nudges that help in pensions become manipulation when serving platforms' engagement.
src/2026/05/12/social-standing.md: People care about rank, not absolute income, per Veblen, Frank, and Hirsch; inequality worsens social outcomes, and social media industrializes status anxiety by replacing neighbors with a global aspirational feed.
src/2026/05/12/workplace-politics.md: Family rhetoric extracts commitment from employees excluded from the winning coalition; selectorate theory and Pfeffer show workplaces are political, so honest participation beats pretending politics doesn't exist.
src/2026/05/13/corporate-psychopathy.md: The Olympus fraud and Hare's checklist show corporate structure produces psychopathic behavior; dark-triad traits are selected for by interviews and impression management in large hierarchies.
src/2026/05/14/manufacturing-obedience.md: Wells Fargo, Milgram, and Volkswagen show ordinary people commit wrongdoing through authority, quotas, and moral disengagement; visible dissenters can break compliance, so few heroes suffice.
src/2026/05/14/manufacturing-preferences.md: Bernays, Veblen, and Galbraith show preferences are manufactured, not given; recommendation systems and industries like Korean cosmetic surgery or Indian skin-lightening construct desire before choice.
src/2026/05/14/regulatory-capture.md: The Minerals Management Service and 2008 crisis illustrate Stigler's regulatory capture; revolving doors and cognitive capture now afflict tech and AI oversight, though Sweden, Korea, and Singapore resist it.
src/2026/05/15/fascism.md: Paxton and Eco define fascism as a style — rebirth cults, victimhood, violence as virtue — and Wilhoit's in-group/out-group logic; examples from Japan, Myanmar, Brazil, and Modi's India show it is not uniquely European.
src/2026/05/15/technofascism.md: TESCREAL billionaires from Thiel to Andreessen treat democracy as an obstacle to their vision; funding Yarvin, Vance, and Gawker's destruction applies reactionary politics through platforms they control.
src/2026/05/17/creating-money.md: Fractional reserve banking began with goldsmiths; the Bank of England confirms banks create money when lending, and fintech and crypto replicate this power while dodging its obligations.
src/2026/05/17/empathy.md: Moral progress expanded via novels, organized pressure, and structured contact, not inevitability; Moyn shows human rights emerged in the 1970s, and every extension provokes counter-movements that can reverse it.
src/2026/05/17/in-a-crisis.md: Disasters produce mutual aid, not panic; Solnit's elite panic explains why Katrina's fabricated looting narratives delayed relief and caused deaths, as biased reporting manufactures mistrust.
src/2026/05/17/not-so-selfish-after-all.md: Ultimatum and public goods games show fairness and punishment of defectors contradict homo economicus; Ostrom's commons refute Hardin, showing cooperation flourishes under institutions designed to support it.
src/2026/05/17/parkinsons-and-bullshit.md: Keynes predicted a fifteen-hour week, but productivity gains became profit and status-signaling busyness; Parkinson's Law and Graeber's bullshit jobs explain expanding pointless work, including in tech.
src/2026/05/18/constructing-race.md: Race and caste are political constructions, from apartheid's pencil test to US census categories and colonial India's hardened jati; algorithms trained on such data cannot be neutral.
src/2026/05/18/representation.md: Uhura changed what futures seemed possible; cultivation theory and Bollywood show media shapes norms, and the representation feedback loop means filters reproduce themselves unless deliberately broken.
src/2026/05/19/identity.md: From Bertillon and fingerprints to Aadhaar, identity systems serve state legibility and often racial control; the dilemma is that registration enables democracy yet has been weaponized against marginalized groups.
src/2026/05/20/glossary.md: A reference glossary defining terms used across the series, from anchoring and enshittification to kayfabe, selectorate theory, TESCREAL, and waqf.
src/2026/05/20/standards.md: Railroad gauges, the AC/DC war, Microsoft's embrace-extend-extinguish, AMP, and Apple's RCS refusal show standards are contested market weapons; interoperability mandates are the structural remedy.
src/2026/05/20/twelve-ways-to-be-wrong.md: Catalogues twelve flawed ways to measure AI coding tools, from line counts and self-reports to selection bias and novelty effects, arguing for rigorous methods borrowed from the human sciences.
src/2026/05/20/why-dont-you-just.md: Just solutions ignore cascading prerequisites, transaction costs, and cognitive load; McIntosh's knapsack and the invisible staircase explain why individual fixes obscure structural failure.
src/2026/05/20/womens-work.md: Iceland's 1975 strike exposed invisible labor; Waring, Hochschild, and Federici show GDP and platforms devalue feminized care work, while family myths and the passion principle make exploitation feel like choice.
src/2026/05/21/enshittification.md: Doctorow's enshittification describes platforms degrading service after lock-in, as with Booking.com, Columbia House, and Grab; the EU's DMA and India's UPI show structural interventions that work.
src/2026/05/26/summer-projects.md: Lists the author's summer plans: teaching workshops on organizational change, project shutdown, and AI skepticism, revising research software project material, finishing a book and a YA novel, writing a Lean tutorial, and finding an agent.
src/2026/05/27/building-a-city-not-a-car.md: Argues that enterprise software is not a product built to sell but a city built to live in, likening it to laying a subway line rather than putting tires on a family car.
src/2026/05/28/basic-queueing-theory.md: Introduces queueing fundamentals: Poisson arrivals, the memoryless exponential distribution, and server utilization, then explains why an M/M/1 queue becomes unstable at exactly full utilization.
src/2026/05/29/queue-formation.md: Derives the M/M/1 mean queue length L = rho/(1-rho), showing queues form even at moderate load because randomness creates bursts, and length blows up as spare capacity vanishes.
src/2026/05/30/how-to-not-be-wrong-about-ai.md: Announces a workshop teaching research methods for judging whether AI coding tools actually help teams, built around an annotated bibliography and further reading.
src/2026/05/30/littles-law.md: Explains Little's Law L = lambda W, its universality across distributions and scheduling disciplines, and how it derives response times from readily observed counts and rates.
src/2026/05/31/ai-research-questions.md: Answers what remains empirically unknown about AI-assisted development with three questions: how developers evaluate claims, whether pre-AI productivity research proved useful, and which desired tools could be evaluated.
src/2026/05/31/sojourn-time.md: Derives the M/M/1 sojourn time W = 1/(mu(1-rho)), showing service time stays constant while waiting dominates as utilization approaches one.
src/2026/06/01/nonlinearity.md: Shows queue length and wait grow nonlinearly as utilization rises, warning that the last few percent of throughput cost enormous latency and to avoid targeting above 80-85% utilization.
src/2026/06/02/pooled-vs-separate.md: Argues pooled queues always outperform separate queues by eliminating wasted idle capacity, often halving wait times, even though separate lines feel fairer to customers.
src/2026/06/03/late-merge.md: Shows late zipper merging beats early merging by doubling buffer capacity and shrinking blocking probability, a structural lesson applying to networks and hospitals.
src/2026/06/04/inspectors-paradox.md: Explains the bus-wait paradox through length-biased sampling: random arrivals land in longer gaps, so expected wait exceeds half the mean headway whenever headways vary.
src/2026/06/05/braess-paradox.md: Shows that adding a shortcut can worsen everyone's travel time at selfish equilibrium, illustrating Braess's paradox, the price of anarchy, and why closing roads sometimes improves traffic.
src/2026/06/05/convoy-effect.md: Compares FIFO and shortest-job-first scheduling under high-variance service, showing SJF cuts mean sojourn time by preventing one long job from blocking many short ones.
src/2026/06/05/priority-starvation.md: Shows static priority queues starve low-priority jobs as high-priority load nears one, and that priority aging prevents starvation but introduces oscillating bursts.
src/2026/06/05/rush-hour.md: Models commuter departure choices as a congestion game in which individual optimization flattens and shifts the peak but never eliminates it, drawing on the Vickrey bottleneck model.
src/2026/06/05/tandem-queue.md: Shows high variance in an upstream stage blocks and starves a downstream stage through a finite buffer, which acts as a shock absorber with diminishing returns.
src/2026/06/08/sex-and-drugs-and-guns-and-code-restart.md: Opens a book of essays with a lesson about listening, then attacks Andreessen's manifesto, arguing technologists must learn how power works to make the world better.
src/2026/06/09/a-little-psychology.md: Surveys behavioral economics and social psychology: people satisfice, value fairness, and cooperate under good institutions, while corporations structurally lack conscience and facts alone rarely change minds.
src/2026/06/09/how-we-got-here.md: Traces the origins of money, the limited-liability corporation, monopoly tactics, intellectual property, standards capture, and how public subsidy becomes private profit.
src/2026/06/10/more-psychology.md: Explores how preferences are manufactured, financial bubbles, the myth of meritocracy, representation feedback loops, and why discrimination persists despite market predictions.
src/2026/06/11/harmful-models.md: Runs a "Big Tech is Like" analogy series comparing platforms to drug cartels, long-firm fraud, enclosure, wrestling kayfabe, the penny press, MLMs, the yakuza, and radio payola.
src/2026/06/12/privacy.md: Argues privacy is a contested, power-shaped good rather than a natural right, showing how surveillance harms development and that privacy for persons should pair with transparency for institutions.
src/2026/06/13/who-gets-what-and-why.md: Applies selectorate theory to workplace politics and the "family" metaphor, then examines racial formation, credit bureaus, tax avoidance, and philanthrocapitalism as contests over who decides.
src/2026/06/14/more-analogies.md: Adds further "Big Tech is Like" comparisons: company towns, the beauty industry, sharecropping, fast-food franchising, Scientology, and ransomware.
src/2026/06/15/what-we-owe-the-future.md: Examines obligations to future generations and the non-identity problem, then warns longtermism is used to justify present harm and concentrated power, arguing we should avoid foreclosing options.
src/2026/06/16/regulation-works.md: Uses tobacco, auto safety, and pollution histories to argue regulation works after organized pressure, and that tech's cognitive pollution needs regulation while misdiagnosed moral panics must be avoided.
src/2026/06/17/how-change.md: Argues that social change comes from organized groups applying sustained political and economic pressure, not individual virtue or moral suasion, citing nonviolent campaigns, the Montreal Protocol, and postwar land reform.
src/2026/06/18/ai-happens.md: Traces how AI displacement repeats earlier automation: firms over-automate, privatize publicly funded research, and externalize costs onto workers, while collective bargaining and regulation offer the only real restraint.

src/2026/10/05/frml-intro.md: Introduces Frml, a small imperative language and verifier built with DeepSeek, explaining how programs become proof obligations that the Z3 SMT solver checks for validity.
src/2026/10/08/frml-scalar.md: Walks through Frml's scalar verifier, symbolic states, fresh variable names, branching, division and assert obligations, and the Z3 check loop that returns unsat, sat, or unknown.

src/2026/10/10/frml-contracts.md: Explains how Frml verifies multi-function programs using requires/ensures contracts, old() snapshots, contract-based calls, recursion with decreases clauses, and forall/exists quantifiers.
src/2026/10/12/frml-loops.md: Shows how Frml verifies while loops by induction: invariants must hold before and after each iteration, assigned variables are havoced, and decreases clauses prove termination.
src/2026/10/13/frml-arrays.md: Describes Frml's array support, Z3 Array/Select/Store terms with symbolic lengths, automatic bounds obligations on access and assignment, and extensional array equality.
src/2026/10/14/frml-builtin.md: Explains Frml's built-in functions, modeling read/write/print as uninterpreted and push/pop as array resizing, while confessing dissatisfaction with special-casing them rather than building an effects system.
src/2026/10/15/frml-complete.md: Notes the final Frml level adds no features, then proposes student extensions: algebraic data types, explicit frame clauses, bit-vector machine integers, and ghost code with lemmas.
src/2026/10/24/whats-in-my-home-directory.md: An annotated tour of his home directory, cataloging source projects, books, papers, and tools alongside the dot-files and credentials various tools have accumulated.
src/2026/10/30/training-data.md: Horror story spanning 1995 to 2025 in which Miskatonic University digitizes an occult special collection as AI training data, releasing something sinister that corrupts models and their creators.
